FILE: backend/core/content/labs/foundations/opt_objective_functions_examples.ipynb
================================================
# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# Objective Functions in ML

Explore common objective functions: MSE, MAE, cross-entropy, and their properties.
"""

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Regression losses
errors = np.linspace(-3, 3, 100)

# MSE: penalizes large errors heavily
mse = errors ** 2

# MAE: linear penalty
mae = np.abs(errors)

# Huber: combines MSE and MAE
delta = 1.0
huber = np.where(np.abs(errors) <= delta, 
                 0.5 * errors**2,
                 delta * (np.abs(errors) - 0.5 * delta))

fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(12, 4))

ax = axes[0]
ax.plot(errors, mse, label='MSE (L2)', linewidth=2)
ax.plot(errors, mae, label='MAE (L1)', linewidth=2)
ax.plot(errors, huber, label=f'Huber (δ={delta})', linewidth=2)
ax.set_xlabel('Error (y - ŷ)')
ax.set_ylabel('Loss')
ax.set_title('Regression Loss Functions')
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)

# Gradients
ax = axes[1]
ax.plot(errors, 2 * errors, label='∂MSE/∂error', linewidth=2)
ax.plot(errors, np.sign(errors), label='∂MAE/∂error', linewidth=2)
ax.set_xlabel('Error')
ax.set_ylabel('Gradient')
ax.set_title('Gradients (for optimization)')
ax.legend()
ax.grid(True, alpha=0.3)

plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()

# Binary cross-entropy
# L = -[y·log(p) + (1-y)·log(1-p)]

p = np.linspace(0.001, 0.999, 100)

# Loss when true label is 1
bce_y1 = -np.log(p)

# Loss when true label is 0
bce_y0 = -np.log(1 - p)

plt.figure(figsize=(8, 5))
plt.plot(p, bce_y1, label='y=1: -log(p)', linewidth=2)
plt.plot(p, bce_y0, label='y=0: -log(1-p)', linewidth=2)
plt.xlabel('Predicted probability p')
plt.ylabel('Loss')
plt.title('Binary Cross-Entropy')
plt.legend()
plt.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
plt.ylim(0, 5)
plt.show()

# Cross-entropy interpretation
print("Binary Cross-Entropy values:")
print("\nWhen true label y = 1:")
for prob in [0.99, 0.9, 0.5, 0.1, 0.01]:
    loss = -np.log(prob)
    print(f"  p = {prob:.2f}: loss = {loss:.3f}")

print("\nWhen true label y = 0:")
for prob in [0.01, 0.1, 0.5, 0.9, 0.99]:
    loss = -np.log(1 - prob)
    print(f"  p = {prob:.2f}: loss = {loss:.3f}")

# Effect of outliers: MSE vs MAE
np.random.seed(42)
y_true = np.array([1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 100])  # Last one is outlier
y_pred_good = np.array([1.1, 2.0, 2.9, 4.1, 5.0, 5.0])

errors = y_true - y_pred_good
print(f"Errors: {errors}")
print(f"\nMSE: {np.mean(errors**2):.2f}")
print(f"MAE: {np.mean(np.abs(errors)):.2f}")
print("\nMSE is dominated by the outlier error!")

# Multi-class cross-entropy (softmax + CE)
def softmax(logits):
    exp_logits = np.exp(logits - np.max(logits))  # Numerical stability
    return exp_logits / exp_logits.sum()

def cross_entropy(probs, true_class):
    return -np.log(probs[true_class])

logits = np.array([2.0, 1.0, 0.5])  # Raw scores for 3 classes
probs = softmax(logits)

print(f"Logits: {logits}")
print(f"Softmax probabilities: {probs.round(3)}")
print(f"Sum: {probs.sum():.4f}")
print(f"\nCross-entropy if true class is:")
for i in range(3):
    print(f"  Class {i}: {cross_entropy(probs, i):.3f}")

"""
## Key Observations

- MSE penalizes large errors quadratically (sensitive to outliers).
- MAE has constant gradient (robust to outliers).
- Cross-entropy heavily penalizes confident wrong predictions.
- Softmax converts logits to probabilities summing to 1.
- Choice of loss function should match the problem and data characteristics.
"""

# Optional: Focal loss for class imbalance
# focal = -(1-p)^γ * log(p) where γ > 0 down-weights easy examples

# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# DataFrame Operations

Master essential Pandas DataFrame operations for data manipulation.
"""

import pandas as pd
import numpy as np

# Create DataFrame
df = pd.DataFrame({
    'name': ['Alice', 'Bob', 'Charlie', 'Diana'],
    'age': [25, 30, 35, 28],
    'salary': [50000, 60000, 70000, 55000],
    'department': ['Sales', 'IT', 'IT', 'Sales']
})
print(df)

# Selection
print(f"Column: {df['name'].tolist()}")
print(f"\nRow by index: {df.iloc[0].to_dict()}")
print(f"\nMultiple columns:\n{df[['name', 'salary']]}")

# Filtering
seniors = df[df['age'] > 28]
print(f"Age > 28:\n{seniors}")

it_high_salary = df[(df['department'] == 'IT') & (df['salary'] > 65000)]
print(f"\nIT with salary > 65000:\n{it_high_salary}")

# Adding columns
df['bonus'] = df['salary'] * 0.1
df['senior'] = df['age'] > 30
print(df)

# Apply functions
df['name_upper'] = df['name'].apply(str.upper)
df['tax'] = df['salary'].apply(lambda x: x * 0.2 if x > 55000 else x * 0.15)
print(df[['name', 'salary', 'tax']])

# Sorting
print(df.sort_values('salary', ascending=False)[['name', 'salary']])

"""
## Key Observations
- df['col'] returns Series, df[['col']] returns DataFrame
- iloc for position, loc for labels
- Chain conditions with & (and) | (or), use parentheses
- apply() for row/column-wise operations
"""

# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# GroupBy and Aggregation

Use Pandas groupby for split-apply-combine operations.
"""

import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
np.random.seed(42)

df = pd.DataFrame({
    'department': np.random.choice(['Sales', 'IT', 'HR'], 100),
    'region': np.random.choice(['East', 'West'], 100),
    'salary': np.random.randint(40000, 90000, 100),
    'experience': np.random.randint(1, 15, 100)
})
print(df.head())

# Basic groupby
dept_stats = df.groupby('department')['salary'].mean()
print(f"Mean salary by department:\n{dept_stats}")

# Multiple aggregations
agg = df.groupby('department').agg({
    'salary': ['mean', 'min', 'max', 'count'],
    'experience': 'mean'
})
print(agg)

# Multi-level groupby
multi = df.groupby(['department', 'region'])['salary'].mean().unstack()
print(multi)

# Transform vs aggregate
# Aggregate: reduces to one value per group
# Transform: returns same-size output
df['dept_mean_salary'] = df.groupby('department')['salary'].transform('mean')
df['salary_vs_dept'] = df['salary'] - df['dept_mean_salary']
print(df[['department', 'salary', 'dept_mean_salary', 'salary_vs_dept']].head())

"""
## Key Observations
- groupby splits data by key, applies function, combines results
- agg() for multiple functions on multiple columns
- transform() keeps original shape (for comparisons)
- unstack() pivots multi-level index to columns
"""

# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# Merging and Joining DataFrames

Combine DataFrames using merge, join, and concat operations.
"""

import pandas as pd

# Sample data
employees = pd.DataFrame({
    'emp_id': [1, 2, 3, 4],
    'name': ['Alice', 'Bob', 'Charlie', 'Diana'],
    'dept_id': [10, 20, 10, 30]
})

departments = pd.DataFrame({
    'dept_id': [10, 20, 40],
    'dept_name': ['Sales', 'IT', 'Marketing']
})

print("Employees:\n", employees)
print("\nDepartments:\n", departments)

# Inner join (only matching)
inner = pd.merge(employees, departments, on='dept_id', how='inner')
print(f"Inner join:\n{inner}")

# Left join (all from left)
left = pd.merge(employees, departments, on='dept_id', how='left')
print(f"Left join:\n{left}")

# Outer join (all from both)
outer = pd.merge(employees, departments, on='dept_id', how='outer')
print(f"Outer join:\n{outer}")

# Concat (stacking DataFrames)
df1 = pd.DataFrame({'A': [1, 2], 'B': [3, 4]})
df2 = pd.DataFrame({'A': [5, 6], 'B': [7, 8]})

vertical = pd.concat([df1, df2], ignore_index=True)
horizontal = pd.concat([df1, df2], axis=1)

print(f"Vertical concat:\n{vertical}")
print(f"\nHorizontal concat:\n{horizontal}")

"""
## Key Observations
- inner: only matching keys
- left: all from left, match or NaN from right
- outer: all from both, NaN where no match
- concat for stacking, merge for database-style joins
"""

# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# Missing Data Handling

Detect, analyze, and handle missing values in DataFrames.
"""

import pandas as pd
import numpy as np

df = pd.DataFrame({
    'A': [1, 2, np.nan, 4],
    'B': [5, np.nan, np.nan, 8],
    'C': ['a', 'b', 'c', None]
})
print(df)

# Detect missing
print(f"Is null:\n{df.isnull()}")
print(f"\nCounts: {df.isnull().sum().to_dict()}")
print(f"Percentage: {(df.isnull().sum() / len(df) * 100).round(1).to_dict()}")

# Drop missing
print(f"Drop rows with any NaN:\n{df.dropna()}")
print(f"\nDrop rows where all NaN:\n{df.dropna(how='all')}")
print(f"\nDrop cols with NaN:\n{df.dropna(axis=1)}")

# Fill missing
print(f"Fill with 0:\n{df.fillna(0)}")
print(f"\nFill A with mean:\n{df['A'].fillna(df['A'].mean())}")
print(f"\nForward fill:\n{df.fillna(method='ffill')}")

# Interpolation
ts = pd.Series([1, np.nan, np.nan, 4, 5])
print(f"Original: {ts.tolist()}")
print(f"Interpolated: {ts.interpolate().tolist()}")

"""
## Key Observations
- isnull()/isna() detect missing values
- dropna() removes, fillna() replaces
- Common fills: mean, median, mode, or forward/backward
- interpolate() for time series or ordered data
"""

# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# Polynomial Features Effect

Visualize how polynomial features enable non-linear fits.
"""

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
np.random.seed(42)

X = np.linspace(0, 5, 50).reshape(-1, 1)
y = 0.5*X.ravel()**2 - X.ravel() + 2 + np.random.randn(50)*0.5

plt.figure(figsize=(10, 5))
plt.scatter(X, y, label='Data', alpha=0.7)

for degree in [1, 2, 5]:
    poly = PolynomialFeatures(degree)
    X_poly = poly.fit_transform(X)
    model = LinearRegression().fit(X_poly, y)
    y_pred = model.predict(X_poly)
    plt.plot(X, y_pred, label=f'Degree {degree}')

plt.legend()
plt.title('Polynomial Features')
plt.show()

"""
## Key Observations
- Polynomial features: [x] → [1, x, x², ...]
- Enables linear models to fit curves
- High degree risks overfitting
"""



================================================
FILE: backend/core/content/labs/foundations/prob_bayes_medical_test.ipynb
================================================
# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# Bayes' Theorem: Medical Test Example

Simulate Bayes' theorem in a classic medical testing scenario to understand posterior probability.
"""

import numpy as np
np.random.seed(42)

# Medical test parameters
prevalence = 0.01        # P(Disease) = 1% have the disease
sensitivity = 0.95       # P(Positive | Disease) = 95%
specificity = 0.90       # P(Negative | No Disease) = 90%
false_positive_rate = 1 - specificity  # P(Positive | No Disease) = 10%

print("Medical test parameters:")
print(f"  Prevalence: {prevalence:.1%}")
print(f"  Sensitivity: {sensitivity:.1%}")
print(f"  Specificity: {specificity:.1%}")
print(f"  False positive rate: {false_positive_rate:.1%}")

# Analytical Bayes calculation
# P(Disease | Positive) = P(Pos | Dis) * P(Dis) / P(Pos)
# P(Positive) = P(Pos | Dis) * P(Dis) + P(Pos | No Dis) * P(No Dis)

p_positive = sensitivity * prevalence + false_positive_rate * (1 - prevalence)
p_disease_given_positive = (sensitivity * prevalence) / p_positive

print(f"\nAnalytical (Bayes):")
print(f"  P(Positive) = {p_positive:.4f}")
print(f"  P(Disease | Positive) = {p_disease_given_positive:.4f}")
print(f"  Despite 95% sensitivity, only {p_disease_given_positive:.1%} of positives have disease!")

# Simulation verification
n_people = 1000000

# Generate disease status
has_disease = np.random.random(n_people) < prevalence

# Generate test results
test_positive = np.zeros(n_people, dtype=bool)
# If diseased: positive with probability = sensitivity
test_positive[has_disease] = np.random.random(np.sum(has_disease)) < sensitivity
# If not diseased: positive with probability = false_positive_rate
test_positive[~has_disease] = np.random.random(np.sum(~has_disease)) < false_positive_rate

# Count outcomes
true_positives = np.sum(has_disease & test_positive)
false_positives = np.sum(~has_disease & test_positive)
total_positives = true_positives + false_positives

p_disease_given_positive_sim = true_positives / total_positives

print(f"\nSimulation ({n_people:,} people):")
print(f"  True positives: {true_positives:,}")
print(f"  False positives: {false_positives:,}")
print(f"  Total positives: {total_positives:,}")
print(f"  P(Disease | Positive) = {p_disease_given_positive_sim:.4f}")

# What if prevalence changes?
print("\nEffect of prevalence on P(Disease | Positive):")
for prev in [0.001, 0.01, 0.05, 0.10, 0.20]:
    p_pos = sensitivity * prev + false_positive_rate * (1 - prev)
    p_dis_given_pos = (sensitivity * prev) / p_pos
    print(f"  Prevalence {prev:5.1%}: P(Disease|Positive) = {p_dis_given_pos:.3f}")

# Confusion matrix for the test
true_negatives = np.sum(~has_disease & ~test_positive)
false_negatives = np.sum(has_disease & ~test_positive)

print("\nConfusion Matrix:")
print(f"                  | Positive | Negative |")
print(f"  Has Disease     | {true_positives:8,} | {false_negatives:8,} |")
print(f"  No Disease      | {false_positives:8,} | {true_negatives:8,} |")

"""
## Key Observations

- Base rate (prevalence) dramatically affects posterior probability.
- With 1% prevalence, most positive tests are false positives.
- High sensitivity doesn't mean high positive predictive value.
- Simulation confirms Bayes' theorem calculation.
- This is why rare disease screening requires careful interpretation.
"""

# Optional: Sequential testing (test twice if positive)
# p_disease_given_two_positives = ?
# (exercise: run Bayes again with updated prior)

# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# Central Limit Theorem Simulation

Demonstrate that sample means from any distribution converge to a normal distribution as sample size increases.
"""

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy import stats
np.random.seed(42)

def simulate_clt(population_generator, pop_name, sample_sizes, n_samples=5000):
    """Simulate CLT for a given population distribution."""
    fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, len(sample_sizes), figsize=(14, 4))
    
    # Get population stats
    pop = population_generator(100000)
    pop_mean, pop_std = np.mean(pop), np.std(pop)
    
    for ax, n in zip(axes, sample_sizes):
        # Draw many samples and compute means
        sample_means = [np.mean(population_generator(n)) for _ in range(n_samples)]
        
        # Plot histogram
        ax.hist(sample_means, bins=40, density=True, alpha=0.7, edgecolor='black')
        
        # Overlay theoretical normal
        expected_se = pop_std / np.sqrt(n)
        x = np.linspace(pop_mean - 4*expected_se, pop_mean + 4*expected_se, 100)
        ax.plot(x, stats.norm.pdf(x, pop_mean, expected_se), 'r-', lw=2)
        
        ax.set_title(f'n = {n}')
        ax.set_xlabel('Sample Mean')
    
    fig.suptitle(f'CLT: {pop_name}', fontsize=12, y=1.02)
    plt.tight_layout()
    plt.show()

# Uniform distribution (not normal, flat)
uniform_gen = lambda n: np.random.uniform(0, 1, n)
simulate_clt(uniform_gen, 'Uniform(0,1)', [1, 5, 30, 100])

# Exponential distribution (highly skewed)
exp_gen = lambda n: np.random.exponential(1, n)
simulate_clt(exp_gen, 'Exponential(λ=1)', [1, 5, 30, 100])

# Bernoulli (discrete, binary)
bernoulli_gen = lambda n: np.random.binomial(1, 0.3, n)
simulate_clt(bernoulli_gen, 'Bernoulli(p=0.3)', [1, 5, 30, 100])

# Verify normality with statistical test
n = 30
n_samples = 1000

# Sample means from exponential
means = [np.mean(np.random.exponential(1, n)) for _ in range(n_samples)]

# Shapiro-Wilk test for normality
stat, p_value = stats.shapiro(means[:500])  # Test works best with smaller samples
print(f"Shapiro-Wilk test on sample means (n={n}):")
print(f"  Statistic = {stat:.4f}")
print(f"  p-value = {p_value:.4f}")
print(f"  Normal? {'Yes' if p_value > 0.05 else 'No'} (at α=0.05)")

# Standard error formula verification
pop = np.random.exponential(10, 100000)
pop_std = np.std(pop)

print("\nSE verification (Exponential population):")
print(f"Population std: {pop_std:.3f}")
for n in [10, 25, 50, 100, 400]:
    means = [np.mean(np.random.choice(pop, n)) for _ in range(1000)]
    observed_se = np.std(means)
    theoretical_se = pop_std / np.sqrt(n)
    print(f"  n={n:3d}: Observed SE={observed_se:.3f}, Theoretical={theoretical_se:.3f}")

"""
## Key Observations

- Sample means become normally distributed regardless of population shape.
- Convergence is faster for symmetric populations.
- n ≥ 30 is often sufficient for practical normality.
- Mean of sampling distribution = population mean.
- SE = σ/√n quantifies spread of sample means.
"""

# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# Conditional Probability Simulation

Simulate conditional probabilities and verify P(A|B) = P(A∩B) / P(B) through frequency counting.
"""

import numpy as np
np.random.seed(42)

# Two dice: P(sum >= 10 | first die = 6)
n_trials = 100000
die1 = np.random.randint(1, 7, n_trials)
die2 = np.random.randint(1, 7, n_trials)
total = die1 + die2

# Condition: first die is 6
condition_B = die1 == 6
event_A = total >= 10

# P(A|B) = P(A ∩ B) / P(B)
p_B = np.mean(condition_B)
p_A_and_B = np.mean(event_A & condition_B)
p_A_given_B_formula = p_A_and_B / p_B

# Direct conditional count
p_A_given_B_direct = np.mean(event_A[condition_B])

print("Two dice: P(sum >= 10 | die1 = 6)")
print(f"  P(B) = P(die1=6) = {p_B:.4f}")
print(f"  P(A ∩ B) = {p_A_and_B:.4f}")
print(f"  P(A|B) via formula = {p_A_given_B_formula:.4f}")
print(f"  P(A|B) direct count = {p_A_given_B_direct:.4f}")
print(f"  Expected: 3/6 = 0.5000 (sum>=10 when die1=6 means die2 ∈ {4,5,6})")

# Card drawing: P(King | Face card)
# Simulate drawing from a standard deck
deck = list(range(52))  # 0-51
n_draws = 100000

kings = 0
face_cards = 0
kings_given_face = 0

for _ in range(n_draws):
    card = np.random.randint(0, 52)
    rank = card % 13  # 0-12: A, 2, ..., 10, J, Q, K
    
    is_face = rank >= 10  # J, Q, K
    is_king = rank == 12
    
    if is_face:
        face_cards += 1
        if is_king:
            kings_given_face += 1
    if is_king:
        kings += 1

print("\nCards: P(King | Face card)")
print(f"  P(Face) = {face_cards / n_draws:.4f} (expected 12/52 = 0.231)")
print(f"  P(King | Face) = {kings_given_face / face_cards:.4f} (expected 4/12 = 0.333)")

# Independence test: two independent events
# A = first coin is heads, B = second coin is heads
n = 100000
coin1 = np.random.choice([0, 1], n)
coin2 = np.random.choice([0, 1], n)

p_A = np.mean(coin1 == 1)
p_B = np.mean(coin2 == 1)
p_A_given_B = np.mean(coin1[coin2 == 1] == 1)

print("\nIndependence check (two coins):")
print(f"  P(A) = {p_A:.4f}")
print(f"  P(A|B) = {p_A_given_B:.4f}")
print(f"  If independent: P(A|B) = P(A) ✓" if abs(p_A - p_A_given_B) < 0.01 else "  Not independent")

# Dependent events: drawing without replacement
n_sims = 100000
first_red = 0
second_red_given_first_red = 0

# Bag: 5 red, 5 blue balls
for _ in range(n_sims):
    bag = ['R'] * 5 + ['B'] * 5
    np.random.shuffle(bag)
    
    if bag[0] == 'R':
        first_red += 1
        if bag[1] == 'R':
            second_red_given_first_red += 1

print("\nDependent events (drawing without replacement):")
print(f"  P(first=Red) = {first_red / n_sims:.4f} (expected 0.5)")
print(f"  P(second=Red | first=Red) = {second_red_given_first_red / first_red:.4f} (expected 4/9 = 0.444)")

"""
## Key Observations

- P(A|B) = P(A∩B) / P(B) is verified through simulation.
- For independent events: P(A|B) = P(A).
- Drawing without replacement creates dependence.
- Conditional probability reduces the sample space to outcomes where B occurred.
"""

# Optional: Chain rule P(A∩B∩C) = P(A)P(B|A)P(C|A∩B)

# Jupyter notebook converted to Python script.

"""
# Distribution Families

Sample and visualize common probability distributions to understand their shapes and use cases.
"""

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy import stats
np.random.seed(42)

n = 10000

# Discrete distributions
bernoulli = np.random.binomial(1, 0.3, n)     # Single trial, p=0.3
binomial = np.random.binomial(20, 0.5, n)    # 20 trials, p=0.5
poisson = np.random.poisson(lam=5, size=n)   # λ=5 events per interval

# Continuous distributions
uniform = np.random.uniform(0, 10, n)
normal = np.random.normal(50, 10, n)
exponential = np.random.exponential(scale=5, size=n)

print("DISCRETE DISTRIBUTIONS:")
print(f"  Bernoulli(p=0.3): Mean={np.mean(bernoulli):.3f} (expected 0.3)")
print(f"  Binomial(n=20, p=0.5): Mean={np.mean(binomial):.2f} (expected 10)")
print(f"  Poisson(λ=5): Mean={np.mean(poisson):.2f} (expected 5)")

print("\nCONTINUOUS DISTRIBUTIONS:")
print(f"  Uniform(0, 10): Mean={np.mean(uniform):.2f} (expected 5)")
print(f"  Normal(50, 10): Mean={np.mean(normal):.2f}, Std={np.std(normal):.2f}")
print(f"  Exponential(λ=1/5): Mean={np.mean(exponential):.2f} (expected 5)")

fig, axes = plt.subplots(2, 3, figsize=(14, 8))
